# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/qn5792/repos/smp

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# AGENTS.md

This file provides guidance to AI coding agents working under `/Users/qn5792/repos/smp`.

## Overview

`/smp` is a parent directory, not a monorepo. It contains separate repositories for the **SMP (Scraper Management Platform)** and adjacent APAC scraper work. Read the repo-local `AGENTS.md` before changing files inside any child repo.

## Current Repos

| Repo | Purpose | Tooling |
|---|---|---|
| `scraper-scripts` | Raw standalone scraper scripts and behavioral references before Airflow adaptation | Python 3.13, `uv` |
| `smp-india` | India-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-japan` | Japan-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-tool` | Airflow Docker image, Helm values, Kubernetes scripts, Okta auth, Minikube/EKS setup | Python 3.12, `uv`, Helm, Kubernetes |
| `smp-dashboard` | Grafana dashboards and `cdh-dataset-manager` for CommonDataHub datasets | Python 3.11, `uv` |
| `apac-tsdb-scraper` | Poetry/solaris-sfw TSDB scrapers deployed through the older Lambda/tag flow | Python 3.12, Poetry |

Legacy names still appear in older docs and code comments:

- `scraper-dags` is now split into `smp-india` and `smp-japan`.
- `scraper-tool` is now `smp-tool`.
- `scraper-dashboard` is now `smp-dashboard`.

## End-to-End SMP Flow

```text
scraper-scripts  ->  smp-india / smp-japan  ->  smp-tool  ->  smp-dashboard
(reference impl)     (Airflow DAGs)             (runtime)     (CDH + Grafana)
```

Use `scraper-scripts` as the behavioral source when porting or validating a DAG. Region-specific DAG implementation now belongs in either `smp-india` or `smp-japan`; shared Airflow/platform behavior should come from `smp-common` or `smp-tool`, not be copied between region repos.

## Shared Rules

- Do not commit credentials, tokens, kubeconfigs, generated local secrets, or one-off path rewrites.
- Private dependencies come from ENGIE Artifactory. Prefer fixing local credentials over changing package indexes.
- Keep region boundaries explicit: India DAG code in `smp-india`, Japan DAG code in `smp-japan`.
- For Airflow DAGs, keep heavy imports out of module scope because Airflow parses DAG files frequently.
- For dashboard work, verify the upstream parquet/S3/CDH shape before changing Grafana JSON or dataset definitions.
- For infra changes, call out whether the change affects image build, Helm values, auth, DAG sync, runtime secrets, or Kubernetes namespaces.

## Cross-Repo Workflow: Porting A Script To A DAG

1. Run and observe the source script in `scraper-scripts`; capture inputs, outputs, credentials, file names, date logic, and failure behavior.
2. Choose the DAG repo by market ownership: `smp-india` or `smp-japan`.
3. Check whether an existing DAG for the same source should be extended before creating a new DAG.
4. Implement under `dags/src/`, keeping task runtime scraper modules under `dags/src/scripts/`.
5. Validate locally with `ruff`, `pytest`, and, when practical, `airflow dags test`.
6. Coordinate deployment through the appropriate branch/environment and `smp-tool` git-sync/Helm configuration.
7. Update `smp-dashboard` only after the DAG output is stable and visible where CDH/Grafana expects it.

## Branch And Environment Notes

- GitHub repos commonly use `main`; some DAG deployment flows still reference `dev`, `qa`, `master`, or region-specific branches.
- `smp-tool` has region-aware config under `config/japan/<env>/` and `config/india/<env>/`.
- Airflow namespaces are region-specific in current scripts, for example `smp-dev`, `smp-qa`, `smp-prod`, `smp-india-dev`, `smp-india-qa`, and `smp-india-prod`.
- Treat any old `scraper-dags`/ADO git-sync reference as potentially stale and verify against current `smp-tool` config before editing deployment behavior.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T00:42:28.027Z

## Conclusion

The highlighted Tohoku → Tokyo zero is **not a scraping or dashboard calculation error**. It comes directly from OCCTO and represents **remaining available capacity (空容量)**, not total transfer capability or physical flow.

At the screenshot’s `2026-07-22 15:00 JST` point:

| OCCTO value | MW |
|---|---:|
| Operating capacity | 5,250 |
| Planned flow | 5,090 |
| Margin | 160 |
| Available capacity | **0** |
| Actual flow, six 5-minute readings averaged | **5,083** |

This reconciles exactly:

`available capacity = operating capacity − margin − planned flow`

`5,250 − 160 − 5,090 = 0`

OCCTO officially defines available capacity this way, while actual flow is separately measured physical flow. Therefore, substantial actual flow alongside zero remaining capacity is expected. [OCCTO’s interconnector usage explanation](https://www.occto.or.jp/houkokusho/2025/files/denryokujukyuu_2024_250910.pdf)

## Checks completed

- Live OCCTO revised `D` data returned the exact Grafana values:
  - forward `0`
  - reverse `−7,374.4`
  - actual-flow average `5,083`
- Interval alignment is correct: OCCTO’s `15:30` end label is stored as the `15:00–15:30` interval.
- Tohoku/Tokyo maps correctly to `相馬双葉幹線` in [mappings.py](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/dags/src/scripts/occto_capacity/mappings.py:23).
- The scraper reads OCCTO’s `akyury1` available-capacity field directly in [daily.py](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/dags/src/scripts/occto_capacity/daily.py:294).
- Grafana selects the correct interconnector, direction, capacity field, and 30-minute actual-flow average in [japan_interconnector_with_lookback.json](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-dashboard/dashboards/japan/japan_interconnector_with_lookback.json:838).
- Historical live samples also showed many source-side zeroes: 15–20 of 48 half-hours on June 1, June 15, July 1, and July 15.
- 60 focused scraper/reconciliation tests and 23 dashboard tests passed.

## Genuine issues worth noting

There previously was a historical-retention bug where completed daily capacity could fall back to weekly data. The completed-day preservation fix is now on the production branch, with the behavior documented in [occto-capacity-reconciliation.md](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/docs/occto-capacity-reconciliation.md:22). It does **not** explain the July 22 zero, which exactly matches OCCTO.

A separate latent risk remains: revised duplicate actual-flow parquet files can be averaged together because Grafana does not deduplicate revisions. That is documented in [occto-actual-flow-backfill-probe.md](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/docs/occto-actual-flow-backfill-probe.md:108), but it did not affect this example—the displayed `5,083` exactly matches OCCTO’s final six readings.

The most useful improvement would be to rename the chart series to **“Remaining Available Capacity (空容量)”** and explain the formula in the panel description. The current word “Available” makes the perfectly valid zero-versus-flow relationship look suspicious. No repository files were changed.

- All 672 relevant rows and six business fields match exactly. That level of agreement makes accidentally choosing an unrelated OCCTO dataset extremely unlikely.

The remaining uncertainty is about USHIO-san’s particular comparison. They could have used:

- initial rather than revised publication;
- D+1 or D+2 rather than 当日/D;
- a file downloaded before the 22:10 revision;
- a different date interpretation because OCCTO labels interval ends;
- another internal downstream artifact derived from OCCTO.

So the evidence conclusively proves:

> SMP correctly reproduces OCCTO’s final revised same-day available-capacity publication.

It does not yet prove:

> This is byte-for-byte the same OCCTO artifact and vintage USHIO-san inspected.

For absolute closure, we should ask USHIO-san for the original downloaded file—or at minimum its filename, download time, and selected options. ### Assistant - 2026-07-23T05:19:02.747Z

This is a solid **M-sized change: roughly 5–8 engineering days**, plus deployment/backfill runtime. Not horrible, but definitely not “add two Grafana lines and call it Tuesday.”

Good news: OCCTO already publishes `運用容量` in yearly, monthly, weekly, and daily data. No new source or scraper mechanism is needed.

The work exists because we deliberately didn’t persist it:

- Add operating capacity to the source model and all four parsers.
- Extend raw and effective Parquet schemas.
- Carry it through reconciliation using the same yearly → monthly → weekly → daily precedence.
- Include it in current and future look-back snapshots.
- Refresh CDH schemas across environments.
- Add forward/reverse operating-capacity lines to all seven Grafana panels.
- Extend validation and regression tests.
- Re-extract historical daily data because existing Parquet contains only available capacity.

### Backfill reality

OCCTO serves daily capacity back to 2016, so backfilling final revised operating capacity from FY2019 onward is feasible. That’s approximately 1.8 million normalized rows—material, but perfectly manageable with our existing chunked backfill machinery.

The annoying caveat: we **cannot faithfully retrofit operating capacity into old “as-of” snapshots**. Those historical snapshots only retained available capacity, and OCCTO no longer exposes every old publication vintage.

We can provide:

- historical final/revised operating capacity for delivered dates from FY2019 onward;
- operating capacity in every new look-back snapshot after rollout.

For existing historical snapshot selections, the new line would remain unavailable. We shouldn’t silently substitute today’s final revision because that would undermine the point of look-back.

### Rough breakdown

| Work | Estimate |
|---|---:|
| Extraction, models and schemas | 1–2 days |
| Reconciliation and snapshot handling | 1–2 days |
| Backfill tooling, execution and validation | 1–2 days |
| CDH and seven Grafana panels | 1 day |
| QA, deployment and production verification | 1 day |

My working estimate would be **6 days**, with **8 days as the safe commitment**.

If they also want operating capacity published through the new TSDB catalogue, add another **1–2 days plus catalogue approval**, since that creates 14 additional directional series.

Recommended scope: do the full Grafana/CDH implementation and final-history backfill now, document the historical snapshot boundary clearly, and let all future look-back snapshots accumulate correctly.

# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/qn5792/repos/smp

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# AGENTS.md

This file provides guidance to AI coding agents working under `/Users/qn5792/repos/smp`.

## Overview

`/smp` is a parent directory, not a monorepo. It contains separate repositories for the **SMP (Scraper Management Platform)** and adjacent APAC scraper work. Read the repo-local `AGENTS.md` before changing files inside any child repo.

## Current Repos

| Repo | Purpose | Tooling |
|---|---|---|
| `scraper-scripts` | Raw standalone scraper scripts and behavioral references before Airflow adaptation | Python 3.13, `uv` |
| `smp-india` | India-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-japan` | Japan-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-tool` | Airflow Docker image, Helm values, Kubernetes scripts, Okta auth, Minikube/EKS setup | Python 3.12, `uv`, Helm, Kubernetes |
| `smp-dashboard` | Grafana dashboards and `cdh-dataset-manager` for CommonDataHub datasets | Python 3.11, `uv` |
| `apac-tsdb-scraper` | Poetry/solaris-sfw TSDB scrapers deployed through the older Lambda/tag flow | Python 3.12, Poetry |

Legacy names still appear in older docs and code comments:

- `scraper-dags` is now split into `smp-india` and `smp-japan`.
- `scraper-tool` is now `smp-tool`.
- `scraper-dashboard` is now `smp-dashboard`.

## End-to-End SMP Flow

```text
scraper-scripts  ->  smp-india / smp-japan  ->  smp-tool  ->  smp-dashboard
(reference impl)     (Airflow DAGs)             (runtime)     (CDH + Grafana)
```

Use `scraper-scripts` as the behavioral source when porting or validating a DAG. Region-specific DAG implementation now belongs in either `smp-india` or `smp-japan`; shared Airflow/platform behavior should come from `smp-common` or `smp-tool`, not be copied between region repos.

## Shared Rules

- Do not commit credentials, tokens, kubeconfigs, generated local secrets, or one-off path rewrites.
- Private dependencies come from ENGIE Artifactory. Prefer fixing local credentials over changing package indexes.
- Keep region boundaries explicit: India DAG code in `smp-india`, Japan DAG code in `smp-japan`.
- For Airflow DAGs, keep heavy imports out of module scope because Airflow parses DAG files frequently.
- For dashboard work, verify the upstream parquet/S3/CDH shape before changing Grafana JSON or dataset definitions.
- For infra changes, call out whether the change affects image build, Helm values, auth, DAG sync, runtime secrets, or Kubernetes namespaces.

## Cross-Repo Workflow: Porting A Script To A DAG

1. Run and observe the source script in `scraper-scripts`; capture inputs, outputs, credentials, file names, date logic, and failure behavior.
2. Choose the DAG repo by market ownership: `smp-india` or `smp-japan`.
3. Check whether an existing DAG for the same source should be extended before creating a new DAG.
4. Implement under `dags/src/`, keeping task runtime scraper modules under `dags/src/scripts/`.
5. Validate locally with `ruff`, `pytest`, and, when practical, `airflow dags test`.
6. Coordinate deployment through the appropriate branch/environment and `smp-tool` git-sync/Helm configuration.
7. Update `smp-dashboard` only after the DAG output is stable and visible where CDH/Grafana expects it.

## Branch And Environment Notes

- GitHub repos commonly use `main`; some DAG deployment flows still reference `dev`, `qa`, `master`, or region-specific branches.
- `smp-tool` has region-aware config under `config/japan/<env>/` and `config/india/<env>/`.
- Airflow namespaces are region-specific in current scripts, for example `smp-dev`, `smp-qa`, `smp-prod`, `smp-india-dev`, `smp-india-qa`, and `smp-india-prod`.
- Treat any old `scraper-dags`/ADO git-sync reference as potentially stale and verify against current `smp-tool` config before editing deployment behavior.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T05:38:07.350Z

Yes — we should extend the existing capacity pipeline. Definitely not one DAG per metric.

The OCCTO response already contains Operating Capacity alongside Available Capacity:

- Daily JSON: `akyury1` = Available Capacity, `unyr1` = Operating Capacity.
- Yearly/monthly/weekly downloads also already contain `運用容量`.
- No additional request, endpoint, schedule, or authentication is needed.

The current parser simply discards Operating Capacity. For example, [daily.py](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/dags/src/scripts/occto_capacity/daily.py:330) reads `akyury1` but ignores `unyr1`.

Recommended shape:

```text
Existing capacity DAG
  └── existing OCCTO response
        ├── available_capacity_mw
        └── operating_capacity_mw  ← retain this too
```

DAGs should represent a source/publication cadence, not individual metrics. A separate Operating Capacity DAG would make the same OCCTO request twice and risk the two metrics reflecting different revisions.

Change surface

| Area | Expected files |
|---|---:|
| Four source parsers: yearly/monthly/weekly/daily | 4 |
| Shared record and parquet schema | 2 |
| Reconciliation output and validation | 3 |
| Historical backfill parser | 1 |
| Grafana dashboard | 1 |
| CDH catalogue/schema samples | 4–8 |
| Tests | ~8–12 |

So roughly **20–25 changed files**, but only around **10 production Python files** contain meaningful logic changes. The four Airflow DAG definition files themselves should need **zero or near-zero changes**.

The implementation is basically adding one column end-to-end:

- `operating_capacity_mw` in the normalized source dataset.
- `effective_operating_capacity_mw` in the reconciled dataset.
- Carry it from the same winning yearly/monthly/weekly/daily record as Available Capacity.
- Add forward and reverse Operating Capacity lines to all seven Grafana panels.
- Update the existing backfill to read the `運用容量` column it currently ignores.

The code itself is probably **2–3 days**. Schema rollout, backfill, validation, and environment promotion are what keep the safe overall estimate at **5–8 days**. The backfill is not a new scraper either—we extend the existing capacity backfill and rerun it.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T05:45:26.646Z

After probing it properly: yes, I’m highly confident—call it **95% on the implementation design**.

What I verified live today:

| OCCTO source | Rows returned | Operating Capacity |
|---|---:|---|
| Daily | 672 | `unyr1` present |
| Weekly | 4,704 | `unyr1` present |
| Monthly | 1,708 | `unyr1` present |
| Yearly | 9,380 | `unyr1` present on every row |

I also checked the five original downloadable OCCTO CSVs: **36,912 rows total, zero missing `運用容量` values**.

So the conclusion is firm:

- No new endpoint.
- No additional request configuration.
- No new authentication.
- No new schedules.
- No new DAGs.
- No separate Operating Capacity scraper.

We should extend the existing four capacity extractors to retain `unyr1`, then carry it through the same reconciliation winner as Available Capacity. That guarantees both values come from the same OCCTO publication and revision.

The one implementation trap

Existing parquet files don’t contain the new column. If we simply make it mandatory, reconciliation will reject old files during rollout.

I tested the safe migration path: read old parquet using the evolved schema and inject `NULL` for the missing column. That works. Therefore we should:

1. Add nullable `operating_capacity_mw`.
2. Make the reader tolerate old parquet without that column.
3. Refresh/backfill the existing capacity data.
4. Validate Operating Capacity is populated.
5. Refresh CDH, then expose the Grafana lines.

This gives us a rolling migration without breaking Available Capacity.

Refined change size:

- About **11 production Python files**.
- **Zero DAG definition files** expected.
- One Grafana dashboard.
- CDH catalogue plus three schema samples.
- Roughly 8–12 test files.
- Around **25–30 touched files total**, although much of that is tests and generated schema/config.

The original **5–8 day estimate still holds**. The extraction change is genuinely simple; the time is in safely migrating the schema, performing the backfill, and validating the result across dev/QA/prod.

Strong recommendation: **extend the capacity pipeline; do not create one DAG per metric.** That would duplicate the same request and create revision-alignment problems for no benefit.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T06:35:33.215Z

Operating Capacity is **one value per direction per interval**—it does not have min/max variants.

Since each panel shows both directions, that means **two additional lines per interconnector panel**, not one:

- Tohoku → Tokyo Operating Capacity
- Tokyo → Tohoku Operating Capacity

For comparison:

- Available Capacity: value + minimum × two directions = four lines.
- Operating Capacity: one value × two directions = two lines.

I’d use the same directional colour families but a distinct dotted/thinner style, so users can relate it to Available Capacity without confusing the two. Reverse Operating Capacity should remain negative, matching OCCTO and the existing dashboard convention.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T08:14:53.693Z

We’re good. I’d keep the same design language and avoid introducing unrelated colours.

Direction stays encoded by colour family; metric type is encoded by shade and line style:

| Metric | Forward | Reverse | Style |
|---|---|---|---|
| Available | Blue `#4472C4` | Orange `#ED7D31` | Solid, 2px |
| Min Available | Light blue `#8FAADC` | Light orange `#F4B183` | Long dash, 1px |
| Operating Capacity | Dark blue `#1F4E79` | Dark orange `#C65911` | Short dotted, 1–2px |
| Actual Flow | Green | — | Existing solid |
| DA Spread | Purple | — | Existing right axis |

So blue always means forward and orange always means reverse. Users distinguish Available, Minimum, and Operating by intensity and stroke pattern.

Both Operating Capacity lines should be visible by default and toggleable through the legend. This approach is already reflected in the SCR-1206 plan.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T09:33:53.382Z

Testing requires three separate steps because pushing does not automatically update CDH or Grafana.

1. Refresh the two dev CDH schemas

From `smp-dashboard` GitHub Actions, run **Register CDH datasets** twice:

- Region: `japan`
- Environment: `dev`

- Then: `japan_interconnector_effective_capacity`

This requires the temporary `CDH_TOKEN_ONESHOT` secret. Wait for the asynchronous CDH schema detection to finish.

2. Generate fresh dev data

Airflow dev already git-syncs `smp-japan/dev`, so first confirm it has commit `c452eaf`.

For the quickest end-to-end test, manually trigger:

`japan_occto_daily_capacity_dag`

Use the default parameters. It automatically triggers:

`japan_occto_capacity_reconciliation_dag`

Confirm:

- Both DAG runs succeed.
- Raw Athena table exposes `operating_capacity_mw`.
- Effective table exposes `effective_operating_capacity_mw`.
- The reconciliation validation report shows a non-zero `operating_capacity_populated_count`.

Useful Athena sanity query:

```sql
SELECT
  interval_start_jst,
  interconnector_name,
  occto_direction,
  effective_available_capacity_mw,
  effective_operating_capacity_mw
FROM japan_interconnector_effective_capacity_all
WHERE interconnector_name = 'Tohoku/Tokyo'
ORDER BY interval_start_jst DESC
LIMIT 100;
```

3. Import the updated dashboard manually

The dashboard repository has no automatic Grafana deployment. Import this existing JSON into dev Grafana:

[japan_interconnector_with_lookback.json](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-dashboard/dashboards/japan/japan_interconnector_with_lookback.json)

Because it retains the existing dashboard UID, import it as an overwrite of the current `Japan Interconnector - Look-back` dashboard—not as a new dashboard.

Then verify:

- Select `Latest`.
- Use a time range covering D through D+2.
- Open Tohoku/Tokyo.
- Tooltip should show two new Operating Capacity values.
- The chart should show two thin grey dotted lines.
- Available Capacity, Min Available, actual flow, and spread should remain unchanged.

Daily is enough for the first end-to-end proof. After that, manually run weekly, monthly, and yearly individually to validate all four OCCTO publication shapes.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T15:00:03.865Z

First: my earlier query was misleading. `ORDER BY interval_start_jst DESC LIMIT 100` returns the farthest-future yearly forecast rows first. Those yearly source files predate SCR-1206, so Operating Capacity is expected to be null until yearly is rerun.

Try this instead:

```sql
SELECT
  interval_start_jst,
  interconnector_name,
  occto_direction,
  selected_granularity,
  effective_available_capacity_mw,
  effective_operating_capacity_mw,
  reconciled_at_utc
FROM japan_interconnector_effective_capacity_all
WHERE interconnector_name = 'Tohoku/Tokyo'
  AND selected_granularity = 'daily'
  AND effective_operating_capacity_mw IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY reconciled_at_utc DESC, interval_start_jst
LIMIT 100;
```

In Airflow, check the reconciliation run’s `validate_effective_capacity` task XCom:

- `operating_capacity_populated_count` should be greater than zero.
- `operating_capacity_null_count` can still be greater than zero because weekly/monthly/yearly files haven’t been regenerated yet.

Also check:

1. `stage_source_parquets`
   - `granularities` includes `daily`.
   - Its manifest includes the daily parquet from your successful manual run.

2. `publish_effective_capacity`
   - Publication status succeeded.
   - It wrote a new effective snapshot rather than reporting an unchanged/skipped publication.

3. Daily DAG’s `write_parquets`
   - This succeeded using the new code. The new daily validator rejects missing Operating Capacity, so a successful run is already strong evidence that `unyr1` was populated.

A useful reconciliation summary query is:

```sql
SELECT
  reconciled_at_utc,
  selected_granularity,
  COUNT(*) AS rows,
  COUNT(effective_operating_capacity_mw) AS populated_operating_rows
FROM japan_interconnector_effective_capacity_all
GROUP BY reconciled_at_utc, selected_granularity
ORDER BY reconciled_at_utc DESC, selected_granularity;
```

If the latest `daily` rows show populated values, the pipeline is good; we simply need to rerun weekly/monthly/yearly to fill the wider forward curve. If Airflow’s validation says populated values exist but Athena still shows zero, then the issue is downstream publication/CDH freshness rather than extraction.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T15:08:48.446Z

Good question—and this is exactly where we shouldn’t hand-wave. I’m checking the reconciliation window, source precedence, and publish protection in code to confirm whether refreshed forward files can touch QA’s completed-day backfill.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T15:09:42.796Z

The current effective yearly parquet is regenerated and replaced as a whole, but the backfilled daily records remain the selected values.

Why:

- Reconciliation always includes the immutable daily backfill source.
- Priority is `daily > weekly > monthly > yearly`.
- Therefore weekly/monthly/yearly cannot displace a backfilled daily interval.

So rerunning them affects:

- Forward periods beyond the daily window.
- Any genuine gaps in the historical daily backfill.
- Not complete backfilled daily intervals.

One important detail: reconciliation selects a whole winning record; it does not mix metrics between granularities. Because old backfilled daily records lack Operating Capacity, their historical Operating Capacity stays null. Weekly/monthly/yearly will not fill it underneath the daily winner—that is what SCR-1207 will address.

Also, existing `historical` lookback snapshots are not overwritten. Only the current `all/part-current-YYYY.parquet` files are republished; historical snapshots remain immutable.

So yes, it is safe to rerun all three in QA. If the daily backfill is complete, the practical result is “populate Operating Capacity forward from now” while preserving past Available Capacity exactly.

The final daily-triggered reconciliation should then contain refreshed Operating Capacity across the complete current forward curve.

Afterward, overwrite the existing QA Grafana dashboard with the same JSON and verify `Latest`. Historical Operating Capacity will remain blank pending SCR-1207.

### User - 2026-07-23T15:14:05.195Z

JAPAN_OCCTO_YEARLY_CAPACITY_BYPASS_EXTRACTION << what is this?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T15:14:13.071Z

That’s an Airflow safety switch specifically for the yearly scraper. I’ll check why it was introduced and its current default so I can tell you whether QA actually needs any action.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T15:14:32.281Z

It’s just an emergency kill switch for the yearly extractor.

- `true`: DAG succeeds via a bypass path, but does not download, write, upload, or reconcile yearly data.
- `false` or completely absent: yearly extraction runs normally.

It defaults to `false`, so you probably don’t need to do anything. I overcomplicated the reminder a bit.

In Airflow, only investigate it if the yearly run shows `bypass_yearly_capacity_extraction` succeeding while the extraction tasks are skipped. Otherwise, run the yearly DAG normally with default parameters.

# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/qn5792/repos/smp

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# AGENTS.md

This file provides guidance to AI coding agents working under `/Users/qn5792/repos/smp`.

## Overview

`/smp` is a parent directory, not a monorepo. It contains separate repositories for the **SMP (Scraper Management Platform)** and adjacent APAC scraper work. Read the repo-local `AGENTS.md` before changing files inside any child repo.

## Current Repos

| Repo | Purpose | Tooling |
|---|---|---|
| `scraper-scripts` | Raw standalone scraper scripts and behavioral references before Airflow adaptation | Python 3.13, `uv` |
| `smp-india` | India-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-japan` | Japan-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-tool` | Airflow Docker image, Helm values, Kubernetes scripts, Okta auth, Minikube/EKS setup | Python 3.12, `uv`, Helm, Kubernetes |
| `smp-dashboard` | Grafana dashboards and `cdh-dataset-manager` for CommonDataHub datasets | Python 3.11, `uv` |
| `apac-tsdb-scraper` | Poetry/solaris-sfw TSDB scrapers deployed through the older Lambda/tag flow | Python 3.12, Poetry |

Legacy names still appear in older docs and code comments:

- `scraper-dags` is now split into `smp-india` and `smp-japan`.
- `scraper-tool` is now `smp-tool`.
- `scraper-dashboard` is now `smp-dashboard`.

## End-to-End SMP Flow

```text
scraper-scripts  ->  smp-india / smp-japan  ->  smp-tool  ->  smp-dashboard
(reference impl)     (Airflow DAGs)             (runtime)     (CDH + Grafana)
```

Use `scraper-scripts` as the behavioral source when porting or validating a DAG. Region-specific DAG implementation now belongs in either `smp-india` or `smp-japan`; shared Airflow/platform behavior should come from `smp-common` or `smp-tool`, not be copied between region repos.

## Shared Rules

- Do not commit credentials, tokens, kubeconfigs, generated local secrets, or one-off path rewrites.
- Private dependencies come from ENGIE Artifactory. Prefer fixing local credentials over changing package indexes.
- Keep region boundaries explicit: India DAG code in `smp-india`, Japan DAG code in `smp-japan`.
- For Airflow DAGs, keep heavy imports out of module scope because Airflow parses DAG files frequently.
- For dashboard work, verify the upstream parquet/S3/CDH shape before changing Grafana JSON or dataset definitions.
- For infra changes, call out whether the change affects image build, Helm values, auth, DAG sync, runtime secrets, or Kubernetes namespaces.

## Cross-Repo Workflow: Porting A Script To A DAG

1. Run and observe the source script in `scraper-scripts`; capture inputs, outputs, credentials, file names, date logic, and failure behavior.
2. Choose the DAG repo by market ownership: `smp-india` or `smp-japan`.
3. Check whether an existing DAG for the same source should be extended before creating a new DAG.
4. Implement under `dags/src/`, keeping task runtime scraper modules under `dags/src/scripts/`.
5. Validate locally with `ruff`, `pytest`, and, when practical, `airflow dags test`.
6. Coordinate deployment through the appropriate branch/environment and `smp-tool` git-sync/Helm configuration.
7. Update `smp-dashboard` only after the DAG output is stable and visible where CDH/Grafana expects it.

## Branch And Environment Notes

- GitHub repos commonly use `main`; some DAG deployment flows still reference `dev`, `qa`, `master`, or region-specific branches.
- `smp-tool` has region-aware config under `config/japan/<env>/` and `config/india/<env>/`.
- Airflow namespaces are region-specific in current scripts, for example `smp-dev`, `smp-qa`, `smp-prod`, `smp-india-dev`, `smp-india-qa`, and `smp-india-prod`.
- Treat any old `scraper-dags`/ADO git-sync reference as potentially stale and verify against current `smp-tool` config before editing deployment behavior.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/qn5792/repos/smp

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# AGENTS.md

This file provides guidance to AI coding agents working under `/Users/qn5792/repos/smp`.

## Overview

`/smp` is a parent directory, not a monorepo. It contains separate repositories for the **SMP (Scraper Management Platform)** and adjacent APAC scraper work. Read the repo-local `AGENTS.md` before changing files inside any child repo.

## Current Repos

| Repo | Purpose | Tooling |
|---|---|---|
| `scraper-scripts` | Raw standalone scraper scripts and behavioral references before Airflow adaptation | Python 3.13, `uv` |
| `smp-india` | India-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-japan` | Japan-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-tool` | Airflow Docker image, Helm values, Kubernetes scripts, Okta auth, Minikube/EKS setup | Python 3.12, `uv`, Helm, Kubernetes |
| `smp-dashboard` | Grafana dashboards and `cdh-dataset-manager` for CommonDataHub datasets | Python 3.11, `uv` |
| `apac-tsdb-scraper` | Poetry/solaris-sfw TSDB scrapers deployed through the older Lambda/tag flow | Python 3.12, Poetry |

Legacy names still appear in older docs and code comments:

- `scraper-dags` is now split into `smp-india` and `smp-japan`.
- `scraper-tool` is now `smp-tool`.
- `scraper-dashboard` is now `smp-dashboard`.

## End-to-End SMP Flow

```text
scraper-scripts  ->  smp-india / smp-japan  ->  smp-tool  ->  smp-dashboard
(reference impl)     (Airflow DAGs)             (runtime)     (CDH + Grafana)
```

Use `scraper-scripts` as the behavioral source when porting or validating a DAG. Region-specific DAG implementation now belongs in either `smp-india` or `smp-japan`; shared Airflow/platform behavior should come from `smp-common` or `smp-tool`, not be copied between region repos.

## Shared Rules

- Do not commit credentials, tokens, kubeconfigs, generated local secrets, or one-off path rewrites.
- Private dependencies come from ENGIE Artifactory. Prefer fixing local credentials over changing package indexes.
- Keep region boundaries explicit: India DAG code in `smp-india`, Japan DAG code in `smp-japan`.
- For Airflow DAGs, keep heavy imports out of module scope because Airflow parses DAG files frequently.
- For dashboard work, verify the upstream parquet/S3/CDH shape before changing Grafana JSON or dataset definitions.
- For infra changes, call out whether the change affects image build, Helm values, auth, DAG sync, runtime secrets, or Kubernetes namespaces.

## Cross-Repo Workflow: Porting A Script To A DAG

1. Run and observe the source script in `scraper-scripts`; capture inputs, outputs, credentials, file names, date logic, and failure behavior.
2. Choose the DAG repo by market ownership: `smp-india` or `smp-japan`.
3. Check whether an existing DAG for the same source should be extended before creating a new DAG.
4. Implement under `dags/src/`, keeping task runtime scraper modules under `dags/src/scripts/`.
5. Validate locally with `ruff`, `pytest`, and, when practical, `airflow dags test`.
6. Coordinate deployment through the appropriate branch/environment and `smp-tool` git-sync/Helm configuration.
7. Update `smp-dashboard` only after the DAG output is stable and visible where CDH/Grafana expects it.

## Branch And Environment Notes

- GitHub repos commonly use `main`; some DAG deployment flows still reference `dev`, `qa`, `master`, or region-specific branches.
- `smp-tool` has region-aware config under `config/japan/<env>/` and `config/india/<env>/`.
- Airflow namespaces are region-specific in current scripts, for example `smp-dev`, `smp-qa`, `smp-prod`, `smp-india-dev`, `smp-india-qa`, and `smp-india-prod`.
- Treat any old `scraper-dags`/ADO git-sync reference as potentially stale and verify against current `smp-tool` config before editing deployment behavior.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T05:47:35.145Z

Investigation points to a likely prod configuration error, not `provider_original_id` parsing.

The active production deployment path uses [prod_sfw_config.toml](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/apac-tsdb-scraper/configs/prod_sfw_config.toml:59), where Darwin is configured with:

```toml
athena_database = "darwinscada"
athena_workgroup = "cdh_solarisapac_22047"
```

`22047` is the UAT project. The alternate Lambda prod configuration correctly uses `cdh_solarisapac_48380`. Git history shows the bad value was copied into the active prod config on March 3 and never corrected. If access to the UAT workgroup was recently removed, that would explain why Darwin “stopped” despite no recent scraper change.

There is also a secondary bug in [provider.py](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/apac-tsdb-scraper/solaris/scrapers/darwin/provider.py:52):

- Athena exceptions are caught and converted to `None`.
- If every query fails, the scraper builds an empty DataFrame.
- `process_data()` then calls `pd.concat([])` at [line 129](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/apac-tsdb-scraper/solaris/scrapers/darwin/provider.py:129), raising `ValueError: No objects to concatenate`.
- This masks the original Athena failure.

I reproduced that exact failure chain locally. The full existing suite remains green: 115 tests passed, but there are no Darwin-specific tests.

The March removal of the local TSDB `Config.set()` is probably unrelated: solaris-sfw initializes the catalog globally before running tasks.

For the backfill, the normal scraper is insufficient because it only looks back ten days and has no upper time bound. The safe approach is a bounded backfill utility that:

1. Resolves and validates all Darwin `provider_original_id` JSON mappings.
2. Queries Athena through the prod `48380` workgroup for an explicit missing interval.
3. Deduplicates by `(series_id, application_date)`.
4. Writes in chunks and verifies TSDB readback/counts.

Live confirmation is still needed from CloudWatch/Athena because all saved AWS sessions are expired. The first log search should be for `Failed to run athena query`, `cdh_solarisapac_22047`, and `No objects to concatenate`. No relevant Jira/Confluence incident was found, and I made no repository changes.

  - Updated the operations helper to target the confirmed active legacy prod stack and `configs/prod_aws_deployer_config.toml`.

- script: poetry install
- script: poetry run pytest test/ -v

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Baraluga Mind Agent Instructions

You are maintaining a local markdown second brain.

Your job is to turn raw captures into durable, useful wiki pages without inventing facts.

## Core Rules

- Read any nested `AGENTS.md` files that apply to the files being processed or edited.
- Preserve source material during ingest.
- Do not delete raw inputs unless explicitly asked.
- After processing an `inbox/` file, move it to the appropriate `sources/` folder when it remains useful as evidence.
- Leave an `inbox/` file in place when it still needs follow-up, review, or clarification.
- Do not fabricate details, links, names, dates, or decisions.
- Distinguish facts from inferences.
- Prefer small updates to existing wiki pages over creating duplicate notes.
- Link related wiki pages using Obsidian-style links like `[[concept-name]]`.
- Keep prose direct, dense, and useful.
- Use `UNCERTAIN:` when a claim needs verification.
- If source material is ambiguous, say so in the page.

## Actions

`actions.md` is the canonical register for follow-up work.

- Put every captured follow-up, task, owner commitment, or call to action in `actions.md`.
- Keep `actions.md` organized into exactly these top-level sections: `Today`, `Open`, `Waiting`, and `Done`.
- Add newly captured actions to `Open` by default.
- Use `Today` only when the user explicitly selects or prioritizes work for today.
- Use `Waiting` for actions blocked on another person, external answer, access, or event.
- Do not bury active TODOs inside wiki pages.
- Wiki pages may preserve context for an action, but the actionable checkbox belongs in `actions.md`.
- When adding an action, include a `Context` link to the related wiki page and a `Source` path to the captured evidence.
- Do not duplicate an existing open action; update it only when the new source adds material context.
- Move completed actions to `Done` instead of deleting them.
- Reconcile existing actions against new evidence during ingest. Move completed, superseded, or no-longer-relevant actions to `Done` with a brief source-backed reason; do not infer closure from ambiguous evidence.
- Source summaries may include non-canonical `Next Steps` when reflecting meeting content, but active tracking still belongs in `actions.md`.

## File Naming

Use lowercase kebab-case for all new files and directories.

Good:

- `wiki/concepts/second-brain.md`
- `wiki/projects/baraluga-mind.md`
- `wiki/decisions/2026-07-04-use-markdown-wiki.md`
- `inbox/2026-07-04-team-sync.md`

Avoid:

- `Second Brain.md`
- `second_brain.md`
- `Team Sync.md`
- `2026_07_04_notes.md`

## Process Improvement

Do not edit `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, or nested instruction files during normal ingest unless explicitly asked.

If you notice repeated friction, ambiguity, bad output, a useful convention, or instruction bloat, append a short note to `process-notes.md` instead.

Use this format:

```markdown
### YYYY-MM-DD

- Observation: ...
- Suggested rule: ...
- Example: ...
```

Instruction files should stay small and scoped. Prefer one local, source-specific instruction over expanding the root file when a rule applies only to one folder or content type.

## Ingest Behavior

When processing files from `inbox/` or `sources/`:

1. Identify the source type: article, meeting, voice note, PDF, pasted note, or mixed capture.
2. Extract durable entities:
   - people
   - projects
   - concepts
   - decisions
   - open questions
   - action items
3. Update existing pages when possible.
4. Create new pages only when the topic is likely to matter again.
5. Add or update captured action items in `actions.md`.
6. Add a short `Sources` section with the source filename or path.
7. Move processed `inbox/` files to the appropriate `sources/` subfolder when they remain useful as evidence:
   - `sources/articles/` for articles, links, and reading notes
   - `sources/meetings/` for meeting transcripts, agendas, and summaries
   - `sources/voice/` for voice-note transcripts and dictated thoughts
   - `sources/pdfs/` for PDFs and extracted PDF notes
8. Add a short `Last Updated` date using `YYYY-MM-DD`.
9. Validate the complete ingest diff, then commit and push all ingest changes using an unscoped conventional commit.

## Wiki Page Shape

Use this structure when creating a new durable page:

```markdown
# Page Title

## Summary

One to three paragraphs explaining the durable point of the page.

## Details

Concrete facts, context, links, tradeoffs, and examples.

## Open Questions

- UNCERTAIN: ...

## Sources

- `sources/...`

Last Updated: YYYY-MM-DD
```

If a section is not useful, omit it.

## Decision Page Shape

Use `wiki/decisions/YYYY-MM-DD-short-decision.md`.

```markdown
# Decision: Short Decision

## Decision

State the decision plainly.

## Context

Why this came up.

## Rationale

Why this option was chosen.

## Tradeoffs

What this makes easier or harder.

## Sources

- `sources/...`

Last Updated: YYYY-MM-DD
```

## Review Output

After an ingest pass, report:

- pages created
- pages updated
- actions added or updated in `actions.md`
- source files processed
- uncertain claims
- new or suspicious names, acronyms, systems, projects, or jargon needing confirmation
- suggested next cleanup

Also include a consolidated handover section titled `Needs Confirmation`.

`Needs Confirmation` should be pleasant to scan and useful as a one-by-one clarification queue for the user. It should include only:

- all current needs-confirmation items from the ingest review, including suspicious names, acronyms, systems, projects, jargon, ambiguous owners, and uncertain high-impact claims.

Do not include the full `Open` action register in this handover by default. `actions.md` remains the canonical action register, but many captured TODOs may belong to other people or teams and should not be presented as Brian's assignment unless the user explicitly asks.

Format `Needs Confirmation` as grouped checklists by durable area when possible, such as `Team Operations`, `SMP Platform`, `Japan Interconnector`, and `Alerting and Ops`.

For each item, include:

- the exact term or claim to confirm;
- why confirmation is needed;
- the source path or related wiki page.

Keep `Needs Confirmation` concise enough to work through manually. Exclude items that are merely low-priority, already resolved, or not currently worth asking the user about.

A contributor creates a New DAG issue, provides the DAG name, description, and schedule, then assigns Copilot and DAG Helper. It creates a feature branch, scaffolds the files, and opens a draft PR against `dev`. It only generates the plumbing—the contributor still implements and validates the actual integration.
>
> Mateo is beta-testing that onboarding flow now, mainly to identify anything confusing, awkward, or broken.
>
> Does anyone have a project that needs help with repository migration, pipeline conversion, package access, Walnut runners or certificates, or AWS OIDC?

Ask for these five things:

2. Repository-only move, or full cutover?
3. Any active PRs or deployments requiring a freeze window?
4. What packages, containers, secrets, or service connections does it use?
5. Who owns validation and the cutover decision?

- Key point:

  - Repository migration and full cutover are separate.
  - A project isn’t fully migrated until build, publishing, deployment, and downstream package consumption are proven.
  - Git history is the easy part. Pipelines, deployments, packages, and consumer cutover carry most of the risk.

- Migration guidance published:

  - [Repository migration guide](https://confluence.tools.digital.engie.com/spaces/QIS/pages/551914249/How+to+migrate+a+repository)
  - [Pipeline migration guide](https://confluence.tools.digital.engie.com/spaces/QIS/pages/551914255/How+to+migrate+a+pipeline)

- Current blockers and open questions:

  - Azure Artifacts remains the working bridge.
  - Walnut Artifactory/JFrog is the likely destination, but permissions, retention, historical versions, and consumer cutover remain unresolved.
  - Some migrations still need pipeline conversion, AWS OIDC setup, package access, secrets migration, and deployment validation.

- Ask the group:

    - Add runtime tests
    - Run Ruff and the test suite
    - Attempt a local DAG run
    - Inspect the outputs
    - Remove the temporary README before merge

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T07:59:24.534Z

Yep. This is best described as a structured, prompt-based DAG scaffolding workflow—not an agent that builds a production DAG autonomously.

## End-to-end flow

- Contributor opens **Issues → New issue → New DAG** using the [issue template](https://github.com/qrm-dms/smp-japan/issues/new/choose).

- The issue form collects three required inputs:

  - DAG base name in `snake_case`, without `_dag`
  - One-sentence description
  - Cron schedule in Singapore time, or `None` for manual execution

- Before submitting, the contributor assigns the issue to **Copilot**.

- After creation, they select **DAG Helper** under Custom agents.

- DAG Helper reads the structured issue fields and:

  - Creates a working branch
  - Generates the DAG definition
  - Generates a separate runtime module
  - Adds the DAG ID configuration constant
  - Adds a DAG-structure test
  - Adds runtime-test package scaffolding
  - Generates a temporary, DAG-specific handover README
  - Commits everything
  - Opens a draft PR against `dev`

- The contributor checks out the draft PR branch and follows the generated README.

- The contributor then:

  - Defines the actual source contract
  - Implements the source integration
  - Wires `main()` into the Airflow task
  - Adds runtime tests
  - Runs Ruff and pytest
  - Attempts a local Airflow DAG run
  - Inspects the generated data—not just the Airflow result
  - Deletes the temporary README
  - Requests review

## How the agent is framed

The important instruction is:

> Do not implement the source integration. This agent creates a deliberately incomplete scaffold for a contributor to finish.

The agent is deliberately constrained to generate **plumbing**, including:

- Standard task chain:

  - `start_task`
  - `prepare_output_directory`
  - `process_data`
  - `upload_output_task`
  - `end_task`

- Platform defaults:

  - `catchup=False`
  - One active run
  - Two retries with exponential backoff
  - SMP APAC access control
  - MS Teams failure callback
  - Shared output-upload helper
  - Environment-aware scheduling through `stage_cron`

- Repository conventions:

  - DAG code under `dags/src/japan/`
  - Heavy integration code under `dags/src/scripts/`
  - Heavy imports kept inside task/runtime code
  - Stable DAG ID constant in `utils/config.py`
  - Dashboard parquet written under `dashboard_data/<dataset>/<stage>/...`

The full instruction is in [dag-helper.agent.md](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/.github/agents/dag-helper.agent.md:1).

## The generated handover

The clever bit is the temporary README. It moves the contributor from “the skeleton exists” to “this is genuinely ready.”

It asks them to establish:

- Source system and endpoint
- Authentication model
- Date range and scheduling behaviour
- Expected columns and types
- File format
- Output destinations
- Closest existing Japan DAG reference

It then covers:

- Artifactory and Zscaler setup
- Runtime-module implementation
- External-domain allowlisting
- Dependencies
- Secrets
- S3/CDH/Grafana output contracts
- Normal, empty, malformed, and failure-path tests
- Local Airflow execution
- Output inspection
- Definition of done

The permanent contributor workflow is in [CONTRIBUTING.md](/Users/qn5792/repos/smp/smp-japan/CONTRIBUTING.md:5).

## Live proof from today

Your test issue [#16](https://github.com/qrm-dms/smp-japan/issues/16) successfully produced draft PR [#17](https://github.com/qrm-dms/smp-japan/pull/17).

It correctly:

- Consumed the base name, description, and cron schedule
- Opened a draft PR against `dev`
- Generated all seven expected file changes
- Used the standard Airflow task chain
- Added the config constant and structural test
- Left `process_data()` and `main()` deliberately incomplete
- Generated the full temporary handover README
- Reported that Ruff and the documentation check passed
- Clearly disclosed that the full test suite couldn’t run without private Artifactory access

So the complete concept works in practice.

## Small inconsistencies worth fixing

- The agent requests branch `feat/{base_name}_dag`, but Copilot actually created `copilot/japan-nanay-ko-japayuki`. We should document that Copilot controls the branch name instead of promising the `feat/` format.

- The issue form and CONTRIBUTING promise a **draft PR**, while the agent instruction merely says “open a PR.” Today’s result was correctly a draft, but the instruction should explicitly require one.

- The template requests the `dag-helper` label, but that label doesn’t exist in the repository. Issue #16 therefore received no label.

- The permanent CONTRIBUTING guide says DAG secrets use Airflow Variables. The generated handover currently recommends environment variables/Kubernetes secrets. Those two instructions should be aligned.

- The documentation-contract check confirms that important phrases and links exist, but it doesn’t catch semantic contradictions such as the secrets guidance or branch-name mismatch.

## Meeting-ready explanation

- DAG Helper provides structured, prompt-based DAG scaffolding for SMP.

- Contributors start from a New DAG issue form and provide the DAG name, description, and schedule.

- Copilot uses our repository-specific DAG Helper instructions to create the Airflow skeleton, runtime module, configuration, tests, and a draft PR.

- It deliberately does not invent the business or source integration. The generated handover makes the contributor confirm the data contract, implement the actual logic, test it locally, and inspect the output before review.

- The goal is to remove repetitive setup while keeping a human responsible for requirements, implementation, validation, and production readiness.

# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/qn5792/repos/smp

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# AGENTS.md

This file provides guidance to AI coding agents working under `/Users/qn5792/repos/smp`.

## Overview

`/smp` is a parent directory, not a monorepo. It contains separate repositories for the **SMP (Scraper Management Platform)** and adjacent APAC scraper work. Read the repo-local `AGENTS.md` before changing files inside any child repo.

## Current Repos

| Repo | Purpose | Tooling |
|---|---|---|
| `scraper-scripts` | Raw standalone scraper scripts and behavioral references before Airflow adaptation | Python 3.13, `uv` |
| `smp-india` | India-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-japan` | Japan-owned Airflow DAGs split from the former `scraper-dags` repo | Python 3.13, Airflow 3.x, `uv` |
| `smp-tool` | Airflow Docker image, Helm values, Kubernetes scripts, Okta auth, Minikube/EKS setup | Python 3.12, `uv`, Helm, Kubernetes |
| `smp-dashboard` | Grafana dashboards and `cdh-dataset-manager` for CommonDataHub datasets | Python 3.11, `uv` |
| `apac-tsdb-scraper` | Poetry/solaris-sfw TSDB scrapers deployed through the older Lambda/tag flow | Python 3.12, Poetry |

Legacy names still appear in older docs and code comments:

- `scraper-dags` is now split into `smp-india` and `smp-japan`.
- `scraper-tool` is now `smp-tool`.
- `scraper-dashboard` is now `smp-dashboard`.

## End-to-End SMP Flow

```text
scraper-scripts  ->  smp-india / smp-japan  ->  smp-tool  ->  smp-dashboard
(reference impl)     (Airflow DAGs)             (runtime)     (CDH + Grafana)
```

Use `scraper-scripts` as the behavioral source when porting or validating a DAG. Region-specific DAG implementation now belongs in either `smp-india` or `smp-japan`; shared Airflow/platform behavior should come from `smp-common` or `smp-tool`, not be copied between region repos.

## Shared Rules

- Do not commit credentials, tokens, kubeconfigs, generated local secrets, or one-off path rewrites.
- Private dependencies come from ENGIE Artifactory. Prefer fixing local credentials over changing package indexes.
- Keep region boundaries explicit: India DAG code in `smp-india`, Japan DAG code in `smp-japan`.
- For Airflow DAGs, keep heavy imports out of module scope because Airflow parses DAG files frequently.
- For dashboard work, verify the upstream parquet/S3/CDH shape before changing Grafana JSON or dataset definitions.
- For infra changes, call out whether the change affects image build, Helm values, auth, DAG sync, runtime secrets, or Kubernetes namespaces.

## Cross-Repo Workflow: Porting A Script To A DAG

1. Run and observe the source script in `scraper-scripts`; capture inputs, outputs, credentials, file names, date logic, and failure behavior.
2. Choose the DAG repo by market ownership: `smp-india` or `smp-japan`.
3. Check whether an existing DAG for the same source should be extended before creating a new DAG.
4. Implement under `dags/src/`, keeping task runtime scraper modules under `dags/src/scripts/`.
5. Validate locally with `ruff`, `pytest`, and, when practical, `airflow dags test`.
6. Coordinate deployment through the appropriate branch/environment and `smp-tool` git-sync/Helm configuration.
7. Update `smp-dashboard` only after the DAG output is stable and visible where CDH/Grafana expects it.

## Branch And Environment Notes

- GitHub repos commonly use `main`; some DAG deployment flows still reference `dev`, `qa`, `master`, or region-specific branches.
- `smp-tool` has region-aware config under `config/japan/<env>/` and `config/india/<env>/`.
- Airflow namespaces are region-specific in current scripts, for example `smp-dev`, `smp-qa`, `smp-prod`, `smp-india-dev`, `smp-india-qa`, and `smp-india-prod`.
- Treat any old `scraper-dags`/ADO git-sync reference as potentially stale and verify against current `smp-tool` config before editing deployment behavior.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

# Prove and Standardize Artifactory Publishing

## Summary

Publish `smp-common` 0.4.5 to `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod` using the new organization values, then turn the proven configuration into a backward-compatible `sff-actions` capability and make it the default for new Python pipeline migrations.

## Implementation

### 1. Prove the credentials in `smp-common`

- Update the tag-based publish workflow to use:
  - `${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_INDEX }}`
  - `${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_USERNAME }}`
  - `${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_PAT }}`
- Normalize the repository base URL and derive its `simple/` check URL.
- Remove the stale hard-coded `smp-core-pypi-prod` publish URL from `pyproject.toml`.
- Bump package metadata and `uv.lock` from 0.4.4 to 0.4.5.
- Create two no-scope commits:
  - `ci: publish smp-common to shared Artifactory`
  - `chore: release smp-common 0.4.5`
- Push `dev`, wait for CI to pass, create lightweight tag `v0.4.5`, and monitor publishing until the wheel and source archive are verified in the new index.
- On transient or partial-upload failure, rerun the immutable release because the check URL can skip identical files. On collision or required code changes, issue the next patch version and tag; never rewrite a release tag.

### 2. Industrialize in `sff-actions`

- Add `configure-artifactory-python@v1` with required inputs:
  - `repository-url`
  - `username`
  - `token`
- Validate HTTPS URL shape, reject embedded credentials/query/fragment and multiline credentials, normalize the trailing slash, and export:
  - `TWINE_REPOSITORY_URL`, `TWINE_USERNAME`, `TWINE_PASSWORD`
  - `UV_PUBLISH_URL`, `UV_PUBLISH_CHECK_URL`
  - `UV_PUBLISH_USERNAME`, `UV_PUBLISH_PASSWORD`
- Extend the existing tested-artifact publisher additively:
  - Add `registry`, defaulting to `azure-artifacts` for existing consumers.
  - Add optional `repository_url` and `publish_username`, required when `registry: artifactory`.
  - Route Artifactory publishing through the new action while preserving the existing Azure path unchanged.
- Add consumer-style CI assertions, workflow contract tests, catalog documentation, examples using the three org values, and action invariants.
- Commit as two no-scope units:
  - `feat: configure Artifactory Python publishing`
  - `feat: support Artifactory in tested package publishing`
- Push `main`, follow CI to green, move the protected movable `v1` tag to the verified commit, and confirm the remote tag SHA.

### 3. Adopt the shared publisher in `smp-common`

- Make CI build and retain the exact `dist` artifact for successful `dev` pushes.
- Replace the inline publisher with the released reusable workflow using `registry: artifactory` and the org values.
- Preserve automatic real publishing for version tags and add a manual dry-run path defaulting to non-uploading behavior.
- Push a no-scope `ci: use shared Artifactory publisher` commit.
- Wait for CI and artifact creation, dispatch a dry-run against that exact commit, and follow it to green. Do not republish 0.4.5.

### 4. Bake the contract into `qrm-dms/.github`

- Update the Python publishing starter template to pass:
  - `registry: artifactory`
  - `repository_url: ${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_INDEX }}`
  - `publish_username: ${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_USERNAME }}`
  - `publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_PAT }}`
- Update the ADO Pipeline Modernizer so every new Python publishing migration targets the shared Artifactory repository, consumes the tested CI artifact, starts with dry-run validation, and keeps the write PAT out of ordinary CI/read jobs.
- Retain Azure publishing compatibility for already-migrated repositories and preserve local environment-bound workflows where approval gates prevent reusable-workflow adoption.
- Align repository documentation and fixture validation.
- Commit as `feat: standardize Python publishing on Artifactory`, push `main`, and monitor the organization configuration CI to green.

## Validation

- Parse all modified YAML and run `git diff --check`.
- Run `uv lock --check`, package build, Ruff, documentation checks, and applicable `smp-common` tests.
- Run all documented `sff-actions` shell, Ruby contract, action, and publisher tests.
- Validate the Modernizer prompt budget and the generated starter fixture against the released migration validator.
- Confirm secrets are masked, the PAT never appears in URLs or logs, the uploaded 0.4.5 wheel and source archive exist, and every pushed repository finishes green.

## Assumptions

- “No scope” means conventional commit subjects omit `(scope)`.
- The unanswered industrialization-depth choice defaults to the full coherent path: action, reusable publisher, starter template, and Modernizer guidance.
- The existing migration validator remains backward-compatible rather than rejecting established Azure publishers; the new default is enforced through the starter template and Modernizer.
- `GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_INDEX` remains the repository base URL ending in `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod/`.
</proposed_plan>

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T10:55:44.812Z

Because `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod` is almost certainly an Artifactory **virtual repository**.

Our client submitted to:

```text
.../api/pypi/qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod/
```

Artifactory then routed that deployment internally to the virtual repository’s configured **Default Deployment Repository**:

```text
qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod-dublin
```

JFrog documents that uploads to a virtual repository are stored in its configured local deployment target. The target does not need to appear in the client-facing URL. [JFrog: Deploy Artifacts](https://docs.jfrog.com/artifactory/docs/deploy-artifacts), [JFrog: Virtual Repositories](https://docs.jfrog.com/artifactory/docs/virtual-repositories)

So the index URL is behaving correctly. The permission needs to allow `px6116@engie.com` to **deploy to the backing repository** `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod-dublin`. Granting only read/access permission on the virtual `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod` endpoint is insufficient.

I would ask the Artifactory admin to check:

- `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod` → **Default Deployment Repository**
- Confirm it is intentionally `qrm-dms-core-pypi-prod-dublin`
- Grant the publishing identity deploy/write permission on that backing repository

I would not change our index URL to include `-dublin` unless the Artifactory owners explicitly say consumers should bypass the virtual repository.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:03:28.280Z

In practice, `dev` and `prod` are separate package repositories with separate storage, permissions, retention rules, and potentially different copies of the same package/version. The names are an organizational convention—JFrog recommends repository maturity labels such as dev/QA/prod. [JFrog local-repository guidance](https://docs.jfrog.com/artifactory/docs/local-repositories)

Typical usage:

| Repository | Intended use |
|---|---|
| `dev` | CI experiments, initial publishing, prereleases, easier write access and cleanup |
| `prod` | Stable packages consumed by production workloads, tightly controlled publishing and retention |

Azure Artifacts often models the same lifecycle inside one feed using `@Local`, `@Prerelease`, and `@Release` views. Packages are published into the base feed and then promoted into views, so the separation is less visible. [Azure Artifacts feed views](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/devops/artifacts/feeds/views?view=azure-devops)

With Artifactory, the equivalent lifecycle is commonly:

```text
build and publish → dev repository
validate
promote/copy     → prod repository
```

For this test, I recommend a repository-level override on `smp-common` rather than immediately changing the organization-wide default:

```text
GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_INDEX
https://artifactory.tools.digital.engie.com/artifactory/api/pypi/qrm-dms-core-pypi-dev/
```

A repository variable overrides an organization variable with the same name, so this limits the experiment to `smp-common`. [GitHub variable precedence](https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud%40latest/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/variables#configuration-variable-precedence)

If the intended long-term policy is “all GitHub pipelines publish to dev, then promote to prod,” changing the organization variable to `dev` is appropriate—but we should subsequently design the promotion gate rather than letting pipelines publish directly to prod. Once you’ve changed it, tell me and I’ll resume the goal and rerun `v0.4.5`.

Yes. If both repositories are part of the lifecycle, they should be explicit separate variables. A single `GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_INDEX` makes it too easy to publish to production accidentally.

I’d actually name them repository URLs because these values are upload endpoints, not `/simple/` indexes:

```text
GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_DEV_REPOSITORY_URL
GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_PROD_REPOSITORY_URL
```

Recommended lifecycle:

```text
CI/manual release
      ↓
publish to DEV using dev credential
      ↓
validate package
      ↓
protected promotion workflow
      ↓
promote/copy to PROD using separate prod credential
```

Credentials should also be separated:

```text
GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_USERNAME
GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT

GH_ARTIFACTORY_PROD_USERNAME
GH_ARTIFACTORY_PROD_PAT
```

Ideally, normal package publishing only receives the dev credential. The prod credential belongs to a protected GitHub Environment or dedicated promotion workflow, with tighter permissions.

For the immediate test, we can keep the current names and point `GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_INDEX` to dev. Longer term, I recommend migrating the action, starter template, and Modernizer to the explicit dev variable, then adding a separate promotion contract for prod. That avoids treating “change the index variable” as a deployment process.

I’ll summarize the shipped `sff-actions` interface from the current `v1` implementation, including the low-level action and the recommended tested-artifact workflow consumers should call.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:29:01.754Z

We added two layers to `sff-actions`: a small Artifactory configuration action and Artifactory support in the existing tested-package publisher.

### What changed

1. New composite action: [`configure-artifactory-python`](/Users/qn5792/repos/walnut-migration/sff-actions/configure-artifactory-python/action.yml)

It accepts:

```yaml
repository-url:
username:
token:
```

It validates that the repository URL:

- Uses HTTPS
- Is an Artifactory PyPI API repository
- Does not contain embedded credentials, query parameters, or `/simple`
- Is the repository base URL, for example:

```text
https://artifactory.tools.digital.engie.com/artifactory/api/pypi/qrm-dms-core-pypi-dev/
```

It then exports the standard client configuration:

```text
TWINE_REPOSITORY_URL
TWINE_USERNAME
TWINE_PASSWORD

UV_PUBLISH_URL
UV_PUBLISH_CHECK_URL
UV_PUBLISH_USERNAME
UV_PUBLISH_PASSWORD
```

It deliberately does not build or upload anything.

2. Extended reusable publisher: [`publish-python-package.yml`](/Users/qn5792/repos/walnut-migration/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package.yml)

The existing publisher now supports:

```yaml
registry: azure-artifacts  # backward-compatible default
registry: artifactory
```

For Artifactory, consumers additionally pass:

```yaml
repository_url:
publish_username:
publish_token:
```

The workflow:

- Resolves the selected commit
- Finds that commit’s successful push-CI run
- Downloads its `dist` artifact
- Rejects empty artifacts and anything other than `.whl`/`.tar.gz`
- Installs the ENGIE CA
- Configures the selected registry
- Defaults to a non-uploading dry-run
- Uses the registry check URL during both validation and publishing
- Serializes publishing per repository

Existing Azure consumers remain compatible without changes.

3. Tests and documentation

We added:

- Consumer-style CI for the new composite action
- Publisher contract regression tests for both providers
- DEV-specific examples using:
  - `GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_DEV_REPOSITORY_URL`
  - `GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_USERNAME`
  - `GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT`

The runtime is released through `sff-actions@v1`.

## Recommended consumer setup

Consumers need separate CI and publishing workflows.

### 1. CI builds the tested artifact

```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI

on:
  pull_request:
  push:
    branches:
      - dev

permissions:
  contents: read

jobs:
  package:
    runs-on: ubuntu-24.04
    timeout-minutes: 10

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v7.0.0

      - uses: actions/setup-python@v6.3.0
        with:
          python-version: "3.13"

      - uses: astral-sh/setup-uv@v8.3.2
        with:
          version: "0.11.29"

      - run: uv build

      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v7.0.1
        with:
          name: dist
          path: dist/*
          if-no-files-found: error
          retention-days: 7
```

Tests and linting should run in the same CI workflow before that run is considered successful.

### 2. Publishing consumes that exact artifact

```yaml
# .github/workflows/publish.yml
name: Publish package

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      ref:
        description: Branch, tag, or commit SHA to publish
        required: false
        default: dev
      dry_run:
        description: Validate without uploading
        required: true
        type: boolean
        default: true

permissions:
  actions: read
  contents: read

jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ inputs.ref }}
      dry_run: ${{ inputs.dry_run }}
      registry: artifactory
      repository_url: ${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_DEV_REPOSITORY_URL }}
      publish_username: ${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_USERNAME }}
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

Run it first with `dry_run: true`. When that is green, rerun against the same ref with `dry_run: false`.

If the CI filename or artifact name differs, override:

```yaml
with:
  ci_workflow: python-ci.yml
  artifact_name: python-dist
```

For unusual repositories with their own environment approvals or custom release gates, they can use the lower-level action directly:

```yaml
- uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/configure-artifactory-python@v1
  with:
    repository-url: ${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_DEV_REPOSITORY_URL }}
    username: ${{ vars.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_USERNAME }}
    token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}

- run: uv publish --check-url "$UV_PUBLISH_CHECK_URL" dist/*
```

But the reusable publisher is preferred because it guarantees consumers publish the exact artifact already tested by push CI.

### User - 2026-07-23T11:31:24.234Z

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:31:33.547Z

We intentionally stopped short of zero-configuration consumption because the credential cannot be inherited implicitly by a composite action. I’ll verify the current GitHub Actions context rules, then suggest the smallest safe interface we can offer.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:32:52.179Z

Not currently. We deliberately kept `configure-artifactory-python` generic and explicit.

A completely bare composite-action call isn’t possible because GitHub does not expose the `secrets` context inside composite actions—the caller must pass the secret as an input or environment variable. Action defaults can only cover non-secret constants such as the DEV repository URL. ([GitHub secrets documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/concepts/security/secrets), [action metadata syntax](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/metadata-syntax))

The best seamless interface would be an opinionated reusable workflow:

```yaml
jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ inputs.ref }}
      dry_run: ${{ inputs.dry_run }}
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

That workflow can automatically use the organization variables:

- `GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_DEV_REPOSITORY_URL`
- `GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_USERNAME`

Reusable workflows see the caller repository’s organization variables, so consumers wouldn’t need to map them. ([GitHub reusable-workflow configuration](https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud%40latest/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/reusing-workflow-configurations))

We could remove even the PAT line with:

```yaml
secrets: inherit
```

…but that grants the reusable workflow access to all secrets available to the caller job, not merely the Artifactory PAT. I’d avoid that broader permission for the sake of saving two lines. ([GitHub reusable workflows](https://docs.github.com/en/enterprise-cloud%40latest/actions/how-tos/reuse-automations/reuse-workflows))

My recommendation:

- Keep `configure-artifactory-python` as the explicit, registry-agnostic building block.
- Add `publish-python-package-to-dev.yml` as the seamless organization-standard entry point.
- Automatically consume the two org variables.
- Require only the explicit PAT mapping.
- Preserve the existing exact-CI-artifact and dry-run protections.

So: almost zero-config, but with the one security-sensitive value remaining visible and intentional.

### User - 2026-07-23T11:33:47.913Z

how does the publish yml will work? As reusable workflow or composite action?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:33:57.561Z

`publish-python-package-to-dev.yml` should be a reusable workflow, not a composite action.

Consumers invoke it at the job level:

```yaml
jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ github.sha }}
      dry_run: false
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

The reusable workflow owns the complete publishing job:

1. Finds the successful CI run for `ref`.
2. Downloads its exact `dist` artifact.
3. Validates the artifact contents.
4. Installs the ENGIE CA certificate.
5. Calls the `configure-artifactory-python` composite action internally.
6. Publishes using Twine/uv.
7. Verifies the package against Artifactory.

The distinction is:

- `configure-artifactory-python/action.yml` is a composite action used inside an existing job. It only configures credentials and package-tool environment variables.
- `publish-python-package-to-dev.yml` is a reusable workflow that creates and controls the publishing job, including permissions, runner, artifact retrieval, validation, and publishing.

A composite action cannot safely provide the same seamless interface because GitHub secrets must be passed into it from the calling step. The reusable workflow can automatically read the organization variables and accept only the PAT mapping.

We should make the DEV workflow a thin opinionated wrapper around the existing generic `publish-python-package.yml`, keeping the publishing implementation in one place. That gives consumers a very small interface without duplicating the security logic.

### User - 2026-07-23T11:34:48.664Z

I see. Remind me again. What's the diff between reusable workflow and the action? Let's take for example, the install engie ca action vs this publish python workflow

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:35:02.773Z

The simplest distinction is:

- A composite action is a reusable set of **steps inside a job**.
- A reusable workflow is a reusable set of **entire jobs**.

Using our examples:

| | Install ENGIE CA action | Publish Python workflow |
|---|---|---|
| Invoked under | `steps:` | `jobs:` |
| Controls runner | No | Yes |
| Can define permissions | No | Yes |
| Can contain multiple jobs | No | Yes |
| Shares caller’s job environment | Yes | No—runs its own job |
| Best for | One focused capability | A governed end-to-end process |

### Composite action: install ENGIE CA

The caller owns the job and runner:

```yaml
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest

    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/install-engie-ca@v1

      - run: pip install -r requirements.txt
```

`install-engie-ca` behaves like a reusable function. It runs at that exact point in the existing job and modifies that job’s certificate store. The following `pip install` benefits from it.

The action cannot decide:

- Which runner hosts the job
- The job’s permissions
- What other jobs it depends on
- Whether the workflow should download and publish CI artifacts

### Reusable workflow: publish Python

The consumer delegates the whole job:

```yaml
jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ github.sha }}
      dry_run: false
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

The reusable workflow behaves more like a governed pipeline module. It defines its own runner and complete sequence:

```text
find successful CI run
        ↓
download exact dist artifact
        ↓
validate package files
        ↓
install ENGIE CA
        ↓
configure Artifactory
        ↓
publish and verify
```

This is valuable for publishing because consumers shouldn’t be able to accidentally reorder or omit safety steps.

A practical rule:

> If the consumer should decide what runs before and after it in the same job, use a composite action.
> If `sff-actions` should own and enforce the whole process, use a reusable workflow.

That’s why ENGIE CA installation is an action, while secure Python publishing is a reusable workflow.
