### User - 2026-07-23T11:54:18.418Z

wht's inputs.ref and inputs.dry_run?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:54:30.136Z

They refer to inputs declared by the consumer’s manual `workflow_dispatch` trigger.

```yaml
on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      ref:
        description: Branch, tag, or commit SHA to publish
        required: false
        default: dev

      dry_run:
        description: Validate without uploading
        required: true
        type: boolean
        default: true
```

Then these values are forwarded to the reusable workflow:

```yaml
with:
  ref: ${{ inputs.ref }}
  dry_run: ${{ inputs.dry_run }}
```

- `ref` selects which revision’s successful CI-built `dist` artifact to publish. Examples: `dev`, `v0.4.5`, or a commit SHA.
- `dry_run: true` validates authentication and package files but does not upload.
- `dry_run: false` performs the actual upload.

A complete consumer workflow is:

```yaml
name: Publish Package

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      ref:
        description: Branch, tag, or commit SHA to publish
        required: false
        default: dev
      dry_run:
        description: Validate without uploading
        required: true
        type: boolean
        default: true

permissions:
  actions: read
  contents: read

jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ inputs.ref }}
      dry_run: ${{ inputs.dry_run }}
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

In the GitHub Actions UI, clicking **Run workflow** displays both as form fields. Normally you leave `ref` as `dev` and keep `dry_run` enabled until you intentionally want to upload.

### User - 2026-07-23T11:55:16.605Z

Ah. If a consumer decides to always deploy on tag... how does he configure that?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:55:28.149Z

The consumer changes its trigger to version tags and passes the triggering tag’s commit SHA to the reusable workflow:

```yaml
name: Publish Package

on:
  push:
    tags:
      - "v*.*.*"

permissions:
  actions: read
  contents: read

jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ github.sha }}
      dry_run: false
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

Here:

- `v*.*.*` matches tags such as `v1.2.3`.
- `github.sha` is the commit targeted by the pushed tag.
- `dry_run: false` performs the real upload automatically.
- The shared workflow finds the successful push-CI `dist` artifact for that commit and publishes it.

One important requirement: that exact commit must already have a successful `push` CI run containing the `dist` artifact. Normally the sequence is:

```text
push/merge commit → CI builds and uploads dist → create version tag → automatic publish
```

If someone creates the tag before CI succeeds, publishing will fail safely with “No successful push CI run found.” They can rerun the failed publishing workflow after CI turns green.

A consumer can also support both automatic tag publishing and manual dry-runs:

```yaml
on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      ref:
        description: Branch, tag, or commit SHA to validate
        required: false
        default: dev
      dry_run:
        description: Validate without uploading
        required: true
        type: boolean
        default: true
  push:
    tags:
      - "v*.*.*"

jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' && inputs.ref || github.sha }}
      dry_run: ${{ github.event_name == 'workflow_dispatch' && inputs.dry_run || false }}
    secrets:
      publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

That’s the pattern currently used by `smp-common`: manual runs default to safe validation; version tags publish automatically.

### User - 2026-07-23T11:56:06.333Z

I see. Lastly, why do we still ask for the publish_token? Can't we set that within the reusable workflow?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:56:17.195Z

Not automatically. GitHub deliberately prevents a reusable workflow from reaching into the caller repository or organization’s secrets unless the caller grants access.

Inside `sff-actions`, this would otherwise be empty:

```yaml
${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

because secrets belong to the consumer’s GitHub Actions context, not to `sff-actions`.

There are three options:

1. Explicit mapping — current and recommended:

```yaml
secrets:
  publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

This grants the reusable workflow exactly one secret. The workflow internally calls it `publish_token`, so it is not coupled to the organization’s secret name.

2. Inherit all available secrets:

```yaml
secrets: inherit
```

Then the reusable workflow could directly use:

```yaml
${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

This is shorter, but gives the reusable workflow access to every organization/repository secret available to that job. It also tightly couples the reusable workflow to that exact secret name.

3. Store the PAT in `sff-actions`

Technically, the reusable workflow could use a secret stored on the `sff-actions` repository, but GitHub evaluates reusable-workflow secrets using the caller’s context. A secret merely stored in the called-workflow repository is not automatically available when another repository calls it. Centralizing it that way would require a different authentication mechanism or indirection.

So the single explicit line is intentional:

```yaml
secrets:
  publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

It’s the authorization boundary: “this publishing job may use this one write-capable credential.” Everything non-sensitive is seamless; the sensitive capability remains an explicit grant.

### User - 2026-07-23T11:57:44.586Z

any con on doing option 2? sff-actions is meant to be ran across org, only for the org, where it uses org-wide secrets/vars.....

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:57:58.909Z

The important nuance is that `secrets: inherit` does not mean “inherit our Artifactory org secrets.” It means “inherit every secret available to this caller job,” including repository-specific secrets. I’m checking the exact current GitHub wording so we can make this as an explicit platform-policy decision.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T11:58:17.653Z

Yes—there are cons, but given `sff-actions` is an organization-controlled platform repository, `secrets: inherit` can be a reasonable policy choice.

The main downside is scope: GitHub passes every secret available to the caller job—not only organization-wide Artifactory secrets, but also repository and potentially environment secrets. ([GitHub workflow syntax](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/workflow-syntax?027eb526_page=2))

So this:

```yaml
secrets: inherit
```

could expose things like repository-specific deployment credentials, API keys, or signing secrets to the reusable workflow even though it only needs the Artifactory PAT.

Other tradeoffs:

- The workflow becomes coupled to the exact secret name `GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT`.
- Auditing the consumer file no longer shows which credential is required.
- A compromised or incorrectly modified `sff-actions` workflow would have a larger secret blast radius.
- Secrets only flow one reusable-workflow level at a time. Our DEV wrapper must explicitly forward the Artifactory PAT to the generic publisher. ([GitHub reusable-workflow documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/how-tos/reuse-automations/reuse-workflows))

For our setup, we could safely contain the inheritance:

```yaml
# Consumer
jobs:
  publish:
    uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/.github/workflows/publish-python-package-to-dev.yml@v1
    with:
      ref: ${{ github.sha }}
      dry_run: false
    secrets: inherit
```

Then the DEV wrapper forwards only one secret to the inner generic publisher:

```yaml
secrets:
  publish_token: ${{ secrets.GH_ARTIFACTORY_DEV_PAT }}
```

Thus, the inner publishing implementation receives only the PAT, although the outer DEV wrapper technically receives all caller-accessible secrets.

My view: `secrets: inherit` is defensible if we formally treat `sff-actions` as trusted organization infrastructure, protect its workflow changes and `v1` tag, and accept the broader boundary. It still cannot be completely omitted—the caller must write `secrets: inherit` to authorize inheritance.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T12:44:26.286Z

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T12:45:06.288Z

Absolutely possible, although our current controls make accidental wrong-file publishing unlikely.

What we already protect against:

- The publisher resolves one exact commit.
- It requires a successful push CI run for that commit.
- It downloads only that run’s artifact named `dist`.
- It rejects empty artifacts and files other than `.whl` or `.tar.gz`.
- GitHub records a SHA-256 digest for uploaded artifacts. ([GitHub artifact documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/tutorials/store-and-share-data))
- `uv --check-url` compares hashes against existing registry files, making retries safe and preventing a different file from silently replacing the same existing release. ([uv publishing documentation](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/guides/package/))

But neither Artifactory nor `uv publish` can determine that the package contains the code we intended. They understand bytes, filenames, hashes, and package protocols—not business intent.

Remaining scenarios include:

- CI accidentally packages stale or generated files.
- The wheel omits an important module even though source tests passed.
- Wheel and sdist report different versions or metadata.
- Someone publishes `master` believing it points to one commit, but the branch moves before the publishing job resolves it.
- A malformed but uploadable archive passes our current extension-only validation.
- A compromised CI build intentionally places the wrong package under the name `dist`.

For `wss-client 0.7.2`, we additionally inspected both wheel and sdist metadata locally, so we know those files reported the correct name and version. However, that inspection is not yet enforced by the shared workflow.

My recommended next hardening layer would be:

1. Run `twine check package-dist/*`.
2. Extract and compare package name/version from every wheel and sdist.
3. Install the wheel into a clean virtual environment and perform an import/version smoke test.
4. Prefer immutable commit SHAs or release tags over moving branch names.
5. For tag releases, require the package version to match the tag.
6. Explicitly verify the GitHub artifact SHA-256 digest before publishing.

That would move us from:

> “These are Python-looking files from the correct successful CI run.”

to:

> “These are intact, internally consistent, installable Python distributions for the expected project and version, produced by the correct CI commit.”

So: pretty safe today against mix-ups and transport corruption, but we can still materially strengthen protection against packaging mistakes.

### User - 2026-07-23T12:46:11.108Z

How do we strengthen this? Add as another reusable workflow? Or composite action?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T12:46:26.507Z

The clean split is: a composite action should validate the files already present in a job; the reusable publisher should continue owning provenance, artifact retrieval, credentials, permissions, and upload. Artifact-digest verification belongs to the reusable workflow’s download boundary, not inside the package validator.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T12:47:14.891Z

Use a composite action, then call it from the existing generic reusable publisher.

No additional reusable workflow is needed because we already have the orchestration boundary:

```text
resolve successful CI run
        ↓
download exact artifact + verify digest
        ↓
validate-python-distribution composite action
        ↓
configure registry
        ↓
uv publish
```

The new action could be:

```yaml
uses: qrm-dms/sff-actions/validate-python-distribution@v1
with:
  distribution-path: package-dist
  expected-name: wss-client       # optional
  expected-version: 0.7.2         # optional
```

It should perform dependency-free structural checks:

- Require at least one wheel and one sdist.
- Reject unexpected files, nested junk, symlinks, and unsafe archive paths.
- Parse wheel `METADATA` and sdist `PKG-INFO`.
- Require every file to report the same normalized package name and version.
- Compare names and versions with optional expected values.
- Verify wheel `RECORD` hashes. The wheel specification requires all payload files to be securely hashed. ([Python wheel specification](https://packaging.python.org/en/latest/specifications/binary-distribution-format/))
- Install every wheel into a temporary target with `pip --no-deps`.
- Confirm the installed distribution metadata exists and reports the expected version.
- Produce a summary containing filenames, sizes, SHA-256 values, name, and version.

The generic `publish-python-package.yml` should also replace `gh run download` with `actions/download-artifact` using the resolved run ID. GitHub says the download action automatically recalculates and verifies the artifact digest against the upload digest. ([GitHub artifact documentation](https://docs.github.com/en/actions/tutorials/store-and-share-data))

That division is important:

- Reusable workflow: commit/run provenance, artifact digest, permissions, credentials, CA installation, and upload.
- Composite action: Python distribution contents, metadata, hashes, and installability.
- `uv`: registry collision and upload handling. Its `--check-url` skips only files that are identical to those already stored. ([uv publishing documentation](https://docs.astral.sh/uv/guides/package/))

Because `publish-python-package-to-dev.yml` delegates to the generic publisher, hardening the generic workflow automatically protects both Azure/custom callers and all DEV Artifactory consumers.

I’d implement this in two layers:

1. Zero-configuration baseline for every consumer: digest, archive safety, metadata agreement, RECORD hashes, and wheel installation.
2. Optional strict release intent: `expected-name` and `expected-version`, with tag-to-version matching when publishing from a release tag.

That strengthens safety substantially without making the normal consumer call any more complicated.

### User - 2026-07-23T13:12:10.259Z

But the second part is mostly on the Artifactory’s responsibility no? So… we shouldn’t check it. Pointless even. If it turned out “wrong” or malformed, is it in our control or in Artifactory’s?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T13:12:18.403Z

Yes — that’s the right boundary.

We control and now verify:

- The package built by CI is structurally valid.
- Wheel and sdist identities match.
- The wheel installs and compiles.
- The downloaded artifact matches GitHub’s digest.
- The exact validated files are submitted to Artifactory.
- Artifactory accepts the upload successfully.

After Artifactory accepts them, storing and serving those same bytes correctly is Artifactory’s responsibility. If it corrupts or mutates them, that is an infrastructure incident—not something every package pipeline should compensate for.

A post-publish download test would add latency, credentials complexity, and potential failures from indexing delay or caching. It would mostly retest Artifactory rather than our package. That belongs in a centralized Artifactory health/smoke check, if needed—not in every publishing workflow.

So I’d keep the workflow exactly where it is now. The ownership boundary is sensible.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T13:25:03.623Z

Exactly: we’ve migrated the write path, but most CI/install paths still authenticate against Azure Artifacts. I’m checking the shared action and migration contract as they stand so I can propose a transition that doesn’t strand packages whose private dependencies haven’t moved yet.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T13:26:23.507Z

Yes. Today we’re intentionally hybrid:

- Private dependencies are still read from Azure Artifacts.
- Newly migrated packages are published to Artifactory DEV.
- Public dependencies generally come from PyPI.

We should not simply replace the Azure URL everywhere yet. Artifactory must first contain—or be able to proxy—the complete private dependency closure.

Recommended approach

1. Establish Artifactory read repositories

Ask the Artifactory team whether `qrm-dms-core-pypi-dev` is local or virtual.

Ideally we have:

- DEV read index: DEV packages + PROD packages + public PyPI
- PROD read index: PROD packages + public PyPI, never DEV
- Temporarily, DEV may proxy the Azure feed while migration is incomplete

That Azure proxy would be the cleanest transition: consumers use only Artifactory immediately, while Artifactory transparently falls back to Azure. Once everything is migrated, remove the Azure remote.

2. Create read-only credentials

Normal CI must not receive the publishing PAT. Add a least-privilege reader identity, for example:

- `GH_ARTIFACTORY_PYTHON_DEV_INDEX_URL`
- `GH_ARTIFACTORY_READ_USERNAME`
- `GH_ARTIFACTORY_READ_PAT`

Later, add a PROD read index. The read URL should be the `/simple/` endpoint and may differ from the publishing repository URL.

3. Backfill dependencies bottom-up

Inventory private package dependencies and migrate foundational libraries first:

```text
common/data/logging libraries
        ↓
client libraries
        ↓
tools and microservices
```

For example, `tdb-client` cannot leave Azure reads until a compatible `web-common` version is available through Artifactory.

Existing Azure packages should be transferred as their original wheel/sdist files where possible—not rebuilt under the same version. Record names, versions, and hashes during the backfill.

4. Add Artifactory read support to `sff-actions`

Once the read credential and index contract exist, extend `configure-artifactory-python` with distinct `read` and `publish` modes.

Read mode should:

- Create a runner-temporary, permission-restricted credential file.
- Configure pip and uv.
- Support container forwarding like the Azure action.
- Never expose the write-capable PAT.
- Prefer the Artifactory virtual index as the single source of truth.

5. Migrate pilot consumers

For each pilot:

- Replace `configure-azure-artifacts-python` with Artifactory read configuration.
- Replace hardcoded Azure URLs in `pyproject.toml`.
- Regenerate source-aware lockfiles if applicable.
- Run CI with the Azure PAT completely absent.
- Test containers and tox environments too.

`tdb-client` would be an excellent pilot because it exercises Poetry, a private dependency, tox, and the `dev` branch.

6. Update governance

After the pilots are green:

- Change the migration contract from Azure reads to Artifactory reads.
- Update the ADO Pipeline Modernizer instructions.
- Crawl and migrate the remaining repositories.
- Eventually remove Azure URLs, secrets, shared-action usage, and any temporary Artifactory-to-Azure proxy.

The key principle is: **move consumption only after dependency availability is proven**. Publishing first was correct; now we migrate the dependency graph from the bottom upward.

The immediate next discovery is whether Artifactory can expose one virtual DEV index containing local DEV/PROD packages, public PyPI, and a temporary Azure remote. That determines whether the transition is seamless or requires a short-lived dual-index fallback.

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T13:32:13.281Z

Your gut is right. If coordinated breakage is acceptable, I would not build an Azure fallback, proxy, or dual-index mode. Those become “temporary” infrastructure that survives for years.

The clean target

```text
GitHub CI
   │
   ├── reads ──► Artifactory virtual Python index
   │                ├── internal PROD packages
   │                ├── internal DEV packages (DEV index only)
   │                └── proxied public PyPI
   │
   └── publishes ──► Artifactory DEV local repository
                         │
                         └── promotion ──► PROD local repository
```

Consumers should use exactly one index. Artifactory—not pip or uv—should aggregate internal packages and public PyPI. This avoids dual-index ambiguity and dependency-confusion problems.

The hard-cutover approach

1. Build the correct Artifactory topology

Have distinct endpoints:

- DEV local repository: writable by DEV publishing.
- PROD local repository: writable only through promotion.
- Public PyPI remote/cache.
- DEV virtual read index: DEV + PROD + public PyPI.
- PROD virtual read index: PROD + public PyPI only.

Do not include Azure anywhere in those virtual repositories.

2. Establish proper identities

Separate credentials by responsibility:

- Read-only identity for CI and developers.
- DEV deployment identity for publishing.
- PROD promotion identity with stronger controls.

Never use the publishing PAT for dependency installation. If GitHub-to-Artifactory workload identity is available in your installation, that would be the best eventual replacement for long-lived PATs.

3. Add the shared read action

Extend `configure-artifactory-python` with a proper read mode, or create a focused read action. It should configure:

- The single Artifactory virtual index.
- Read-only authentication.
- pip and uv.
- tox child environments.
- Docker/container credential and CA forwarding.

No Azure fallback and no second index.

4. Re-release the internal package graph

I would avoid bulk-copying historical Azure artifacts unless a version is still operationally required.

For actively required packages:

- Start with foundational packages having no internal dependencies.
- Build them through GitHub CI.
- Publish fresh patch versions to Artifactory DEV.
- Move upward through client libraries, tools, then services.
- Update dependents to those freshly published versions.

For example:

```text
web-common 2.x+1
    ↓
tdb-client 4.1.1
    ↓
services consuming tdb-client
```

This gives every migrated artifact the provenance and validation contract we just established. We avoid importing years of opaque baggage from Azure.

5. Cut consumers over one layer at a time

A repository changes only after its complete private dependency closure exists in Artifactory.

Its migration commit should simultaneously:

- Replace Azure read configuration with Artifactory.
- Remove Azure URLs from `pyproject.toml`, tox, scripts, and Docker configuration.
- Update internal dependency versions.
- Regenerate source-aware lockfiles.
- Build, test, and publish using Artifactory exclusively.

That repository may be broken briefly during the coordinated wave, but it never enters a dual-feed state.

6. Enforce the final state

Update the migration contract and Modernizer to require:

- Artifactory read configuration.
- Artifactory DEV publishing.
- No `pkgs.dev.azure.com` references.
- No Azure Artifacts PAT references.
- One approved virtual index.
- Separate read and write credentials.

Add an organization crawl/check that fails if Azure package-feed references are reintroduced.

7. Delete the old path

Once the graph is green:

- Remove Azure package secrets.
- Retire `configure-azure-artifacts-python`.
- Remove the Azure publisher option from the generic workflow.
- Make Artifactory the only supported Python package system.
- Freeze and eventually remove the Azure feed.

My recommendation

Treat this as a short, coordinated ecosystem migration:

1. Artifactory virtual read indexes and read credential.
2. Shared read action.
3. Inventory dependency graph.
4. Freshly publish foundation packages.
5. Migrate upward layer by layer.
6. Enforce “zero Azure references.”
7. Retire Azure completely.

That is a hard cutover, but it is structurally clean: no proxy, no fallback, no duplicated resolver behavior, and no temporary abstraction we later have to unwind.

### User - 2026-07-23T14:43:08.810Z

Wait, explain to me again why some are blocked, deferred, and skipped?

### Assistant - 2026-07-23T14:43:30.075Z

The categories answer different questions:

- Blocked: “This should be published, but a required release gate cannot currently run.”
- Deferred: “This should likely be published, but another internal package must come first.”
- Skipped: “Repository evidence does not show that this is an Artifactory distribution.”

### Blocked

`sff-data-types` and `sff-tool-or-common` are genuine published Python distributions. Both have explicit Twine-based Azure release pipelines.

Their tests depend on proprietary GAMS resources that GitHub Actions cannot currently access:

- GAMS installers stored as Azure Universal Packages.
- A GAMS licence stored as an Azure secure file.
- In `sff-data-types`, a substantial Windows/Linux and Python-version matrix built around those resources.

We only had Artifactory Python credentials—not equivalent GAMS assets or secrets. Publishing without those tests would weaken the established release gate, while copying the licence or inventing another download mechanism would be unsafe. Therefore they are blocked pending a GitHub-accessible GAMS installer/licence contract.

### Deferred

`sff-fw-limma` is a real distribution with historical publication evidence, but the fresh `setup.py` crawl found:

```text
python-datatypes>=1.1.0
```

That package comes from blocked `sff-data-types`. Migrating Limma now would leave it dependent on an unpublished private SFF package or force an Azure fallback, which was explicitly prohibited.

So Limma is deferred until `python-datatypes` is published to Artifactory.

### Skipped

These repositories lacked convincing evidence that their Python metadata represents a separately published distribution:

- `sff-doc-or`: documentation/example repository; no release pipeline or history.
- `sff-template-test-or`: project template with test configuration, not a reusable package release.
- `sff-tool-rolemaker`: desktop/CLI application with test CI only.
- `sff-tool-kubestat`: single-file operational utility without a build backend or release process.
- `sff-tool-certificate`: installable CLI metadata, but no CI, changelog, publication workflow, or registry history.
- `sff-tool-cmdutil`: same—locally installable does not establish registry publication intent.
- `sff-tool-kube-monitoring`: operational configuration repository with committed local egg metadata, but no release evidence.
- `sff-lib-file-manager`: deployed FastAPI application with container/infrastructure workflows.
- `sff-iam-federation`: deployable AWS CDK application, not a shared library release.

The important distinction is that a `pyproject.toml`, package directory, or editable installation capability is not enough. Automatically publishing those repositories would invent product/release intent that their histories do not demonstrate.

All decisions and source evidence are recorded in the [publishing-order document](/Users/qn5792/repos/walnut-migration/sff-actions/docs/python-artifactory-publishing-order.md).

# AGENTS.md instructions for /Users/qn5792/baraluga-mind

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Baraluga Mind Agent Instructions

You are maintaining a local markdown second brain.

Your job is to turn raw captures into durable, useful wiki pages without inventing facts.

## Core Rules

- Read any nested `AGENTS.md` files that apply to the files being processed or edited.
- Preserve source material during ingest.
- Do not delete raw inputs unless explicitly asked.
- After processing an `inbox/` file, move it to the appropriate `sources/` folder when it remains useful as evidence.
- Leave an `inbox/` file in place when it still needs follow-up, review, or clarification.
- Do not fabricate details, links, names, dates, or decisions.
- Distinguish facts from inferences.
- Prefer small updates to existing wiki pages over creating duplicate notes.
- Link related wiki pages using Obsidian-style links like `[[concept-name]]`.
- Keep prose direct, dense, and useful.
- Use `UNCERTAIN:` when a claim needs verification.
- If source material is ambiguous, say so in the page.

## Actions

`actions.md` is the canonical register for follow-up work.

- Put every captured follow-up, task, owner commitment, or call to action in `actions.md`.
- Keep `actions.md` organized into exactly these top-level sections: `Today`, `Open`, `Waiting`, and `Done`.
- Add newly captured actions to `Open` by default.
- Use `Today` only when the user explicitly selects or prioritizes work for today.
- Use `Waiting` for actions blocked on another person, external answer, access, or event.
- Do not bury active TODOs inside wiki pages.
- Wiki pages may preserve context for an action, but the actionable checkbox belongs in `actions.md`.
- When adding an action, include a `Context` link to the related wiki page and a `Source` path to the captured evidence.
- Do not duplicate an existing open action; update it only when the new source adds material context.
- Move completed actions to `Done` instead of deleting them.
- Reconcile existing actions against new evidence during ingest. Move completed, superseded, or no-longer-relevant actions to `Done` with a brief source-backed reason; do not infer closure from ambiguous evidence.
- Source summaries may include non-canonical `Next Steps` when reflecting meeting content, but active tracking still belongs in `actions.md`.

## File Naming

Use lowercase kebab-case for all new files and directories.

Good:

- `wiki/concepts/second-brain.md`
- `wiki/projects/baraluga-mind.md`
- `wiki/decisions/2026-07-04-use-markdown-wiki.md`
- `inbox/2026-07-04-team-sync.md`

Avoid:

- `Second Brain.md`
- `second_brain.md`
- `Team Sync.md`
- `2026_07_04_notes.md`

## Process Improvement

Do not edit `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, or nested instruction files during normal ingest unless explicitly asked.

If you notice repeated friction, ambiguity, bad output, a useful convention, or instruction bloat, append a short note to `process-notes.md` instead.

Use this format:

```markdown
### YYYY-MM-DD

- Observation: ...
- Suggested rule: ...
- Example: ...
```

Instruction files should stay small and scoped. Prefer one local, source-specific instruction over expanding the root file when a rule applies only to one folder or content type.

## Ingest Behavior

When processing files from `inbox/` or `sources/`:

1. Identify the source type: article, meeting, voice note, PDF, pasted note, or mixed capture.
2. Extract durable entities:
   - people
   - projects
   - concepts
   - decisions
   - open questions
   - action items
3. Update existing pages when possible.
4. Create new pages only when the topic is likely to matter again.
5. Add or update captured action items in `actions.md`.
6. Add a short `Sources` section with the source filename or path.
7. Move processed `inbox/` files to the appropriate `sources/` subfolder when they remain useful as evidence:
   - `sources/articles/` for articles, links, and reading notes
   - `sources/meetings/` for meeting transcripts, agendas, and summaries
   - `sources/voice/` for voice-note transcripts and dictated thoughts
   - `sources/pdfs/` for PDFs and extracted PDF notes
8. Add a short `Last Updated` date using `YYYY-MM-DD`.
9. Validate the complete ingest diff, then commit and push all ingest changes using an unscoped conventional commit.

## Wiki Page Shape

Use this structure when creating a new durable page:

```markdown
# Page Title

## Summary

One to three paragraphs explaining the durable point of the page.

## Details

Concrete facts, context, links, tradeoffs, and examples.

## Open Questions

- UNCERTAIN: ...

## Sources

- `sources/...`

Last Updated: YYYY-MM-DD
```

If a section is not useful, omit it.

## Decision Page Shape

Use `wiki/decisions/YYYY-MM-DD-short-decision.md`.

```markdown
# Decision: Short Decision

## Decision

State the decision plainly.

## Context

Why this came up.

## Rationale

Why this option was chosen.

## Tradeoffs

What this makes easier or harder.

## Sources

- `sources/...`

Last Updated: YYYY-MM-DD
```

## Review Output

After an ingest pass, report:

- pages created
- pages updated
- actions added or updated in `actions.md`
- source files processed
- uncertain claims
- new or suspicious names, acronyms, systems, projects, or jargon needing confirmation
- suggested next cleanup

Also include a consolidated handover section titled `Needs Confirmation`.

`Needs Confirmation` should be pleasant to scan and useful as a one-by-one clarification queue for the user. It should include only:

- all current needs-confirmation items from the ingest review, including suspicious names, acronyms, systems, projects, jargon, ambiguous owners, and uncertain high-impact claims.

Do not include the full `Open` action register in this handover by default. `actions.md` remains the canonical action register, but many captured TODOs may belong to other people or teams and should not be presented as Brian's assignment unless the user explicitly asks.

Format `Needs Confirmation` as grouped checklists by durable area when possible, such as `Team Operations`, `SMP Platform`, `Japan Interconnector`, and `Alerting and Ops`.

For each item, include:

- the exact term or claim to confirm;
- why confirmation is needed;
- the source path or related wiki page.

Keep `Needs Confirmation` concise enough to work through manually. Exclude items that are merely low-priority, already resolved, or not currently worth asking the user about.

</INSTRUCTIONS>

<INSTRUCTIONS>
# Baraluga Mind Agent Instructions

You are maintaining a local markdown second brain.

Your job is to turn raw captures into durable, useful wiki pages without inventing facts.

## Core Rules

- Read any nested `AGENTS.md` files that apply to the files being processed or edited.
- Preserve source material during ingest.
- Do not delete raw inputs unless explicitly asked.
- After processing an `inbox/` file, move it to the appropriate `sources/` folder when it remains useful as evidence.
- Leave an `inbox/` file in place when it still needs follow-up, review, or clarification.
- Do not fabricate details, links, names, dates, or decisions.
- Distinguish facts from inferences.
- Prefer small updates to existing wiki pages over creating duplicate notes.
- Link related wiki pages using Obsidian-style links like `[[concept-name]]`.
- Keep prose direct, dense, and useful.
- Use `UNCERTAIN:` when a claim needs verification.
- If source material is ambiguous, say so in the page.

## Actions

`actions.md` is the canonical register for follow-up work.

- Put every captured follow-up, task, owner commitment, or call to action in `actions.md`.
- Keep `actions.md` organized into exactly these top-level sections: `Today`, `Open`, `Waiting`, and `Done`.
- Add newly captured actions to `Open` by default.
- Use `Today` only when the user explicitly selects or prioritizes work for today.
- Use `Waiting` for actions blocked on another person, external answer, access, or event.
- Do not bury active TODOs inside wiki pages.
- Wiki pages may preserve context for an action, but the actionable checkbox belongs in `actions.md`.
- When adding an action, include a `Context` link to the related wiki page and a `Source` path to the captured evidence.
- Do not duplicate an existing open action; update it only when the new source adds material context.
- Move completed actions to `Done` instead of deleting them.
- Reconcile existing actions against new evidence during ingest. Move completed, superseded, or no-longer-relevant actions to `Done` with a brief source-backed reason; do not infer closure from ambiguous evidence.
- Source summaries may include non-canonical `Next Steps` when reflecting meeting content, but active tracking still belongs in `actions.md`.

## File Naming

Use lowercase kebab-case for all new files and directories.

Good:

- `wiki/concepts/second-brain.md`
- `wiki/projects/baraluga-mind.md`
- `wiki/decisions/2026-07-04-use-markdown-wiki.md`
- `inbox/2026-07-04-team-sync.md`

Avoid:

- `Second Brain.md`
- `second_brain.md`
- `Team Sync.md`
- `2026_07_04_notes.md`

## Process Improvement

Do not edit `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, or nested instruction files during normal ingest unless explicitly asked.

If you notice repeated friction, ambiguity, bad output, a useful convention, or instruction bloat, append a short note to `process-notes.md` instead.

Use this format:

```markdown
### YYYY-MM-DD

- Observation: ...
- Suggested rule: ...
- Example: ...
```

Instruction files should stay small and scoped. Prefer one local, source-specific instruction over expanding the root file when a rule applies only to one folder or content type.

## Ingest Behavior

When processing files from `inbox/` or `sources/`:

1. Identify the source type: article, meeting, voice note, PDF, pasted note, or mixed capture.
2. Extract durable entities:
   - people
   - projects
   - concepts
   - decisions
   - open questions
   - action items
3. Update existing pages when possible.
4. Create new pages only when the topic is likely to matter again.
5. Add or update captured action items in `actions.md`.
6. Add a short `Sources` section with the source filename or path.
7. Move processed `inbox/` files to the appropriate `sources/` subfolder when they remain useful as evidence:
   - `sources/articles/` for articles, links, and reading notes
   - `sources/meetings/` for meeting transcripts, agendas, and summaries
   - `sources/voice/` for voice-note transcripts and dictated thoughts
   - `sources/pdfs/` for PDFs and extracted PDF notes
8. Add a short `Last Updated` date using `YYYY-MM-DD`.
9. Validate the complete ingest diff, then commit and push all ingest changes using an unscoped conventional commit.

## Wiki Page Shape

Use this structure when creating a new durable page:

```markdown
# Page Title

## Summary

One to three paragraphs explaining the durable point of the page.

## Details

Concrete facts, context, links, tradeoffs, and examples.

## Open Questions

- UNCERTAIN: ...

## Sources

- `sources/...`

Last Updated: YYYY-MM-DD
```

If a section is not useful, omit it.

## Decision Page Shape

Use `wiki/decisions/YYYY-MM-DD-short-decision.md`.

```markdown
# Decision: Short Decision

## Decision

State the decision plainly.

## Context

Why this came up.

## Rationale

Why this option was chosen.

## Tradeoffs

What this makes easier or harder.

## Sources

- `sources/...`

Last Updated: YYYY-MM-DD
```

## Review Output

After an ingest pass, report:

- pages created
- pages updated
- actions added or updated in `actions.md`
- source files processed
- uncertain claims
- new or suspicious names, acronyms, systems, projects, or jargon needing confirmation
- suggested next cleanup

Also include a consolidated handover section titled `Needs Confirmation`.

`Needs Confirmation` should be pleasant to scan and useful as a one-by-one clarification queue for the user. It should include only:

Do not include the full `Open` action register in this handover by default. `actions.md` remains the canonical action register, but many captured TODOs may belong to other people or teams and should not be presented as Brian's assignment unless the user explicitly asks.

Format `Needs Confirmation` as grouped checklists by durable area when possible, such as `Team Operations`, `SMP Platform`, `Japan Interconnector`, and `Alerting and Ops`.

For each item, include:

- the exact term or claim to confirm;
- why confirmation is needed;
- the source path or related wiki page.

Keep `Needs Confirmation` concise enough to work through manually. Exclude items that are merely low-priority, already resolved, or not currently worth asking the user about.
